Beautiful, interactive, real-time dashboards. But here's the uncomfortable truth →\n\nMost of them are never acted upon.\n\nThe 2024 shift I'm seeing across enterprises:\n\n1. Descriptive Analytics (what happened) is now table stakes\n2. Diagnostic Analytics (why it happened) is becoming automated\n3. Predictive Analytics (what will happen) is getting commoditized\n4. Prescriptive Analytics (what should we do) is where the real value lives\n\nThe gap? Most teams are still stuck at steps 1 and 2 while the business is asking for step 4.\n\nThis is where Decision Intelligence comes in. It's not about better dashboards — it's about connecting data directly to decisions. Embedding ML models into operational workflows. Triggering actions automatically when patterns emerge.\n\nI recently worked with a logistics company that had 47 dashboards tracking fleet performance. Yet it took them 3 days to notice a 12% drop in delivery efficiency.\n\nWe replaced 80% of those dashboards with an automated decision engine. When efficiency drops below 5%, the system identifies the root cause (weather, routing, driver), simulates corrective actions, and recommends the optimal path.\n\nResult? Response time went from 3 days to 37 minutes.\n\nThe shift happening right now: Data teams are evolving from report providers to decision architects.\n\nIs your team building reports or building decision systems? And people are slow, biased, and distracted.\n\nThe industry trend I'm watching closely in 2024-2025: the shift from passive analytics to autonomous decision intelligence.\n\nHere's what's changing:\n\n1. From Reactive to Predictive → Companies are moving beyond 'what happened' to 'what will happen.' But the real leap is skipping straight to 'what should we do about it?'\n\n2. From Dashboards to Actions → Instead of a dashboard alerting you that inventory is low, systems auto-trigger reorders. Instead of flagging churn risk, they auto-send retention offers.\n\n3. From Human-in-the-Loop to Human-on-the-Loop → Humans shift from being the decision-maker to being the exception-handler. The system handles 90% of routine decisions; humans oversee the edge cases.\n\n4. From SQL Queries to Natural Language → The next generation of analysts won't need to write complex CTEs. They'll ask 'why did Q3 revenue dip in EMEA?' and get a plain-English answer with supporting logic they can verify.\n\nThe companies winning right now aren't the ones with the best dashboards. Here's what's replacing it.\n\nFor the last 18 months, most teams have treated AI like an accessory — bolt a chatbot onto your existing stack and call it a day. But the real shift happening right now is far more fundamental.\n\nWe're watching the rise of data infrastructure that thinks.\n\n3 trends colliding to make this possible:\n\n1. The unbundling of the monolithic data platform. Teams are moving away from single-vendor stacks toward composable architectures — best-of-breed tools that talk to each other via open protocols (Iceberg, Arrow Flight, Delta Sharing).\n\n2. Embedded AI agents are replacing middleware. Instead of Airflow DAGs that blindly move data on a schedule, we're seeing agent-based pipelines that introspect schemas, detect drift, auto-clean corrupt records, and decide where to route data in real-time. Not AI wrapped around the pipeline — AI inside the pipeline.\n\n3. The metadata layer is becoming the control plane. Tools like OpenMetadata, Atlan, and DataHub are evolving from catalogues into active runtime orchestrators. They don't just describe what data exists — they enforce quality rules, trigger transformations, and govern access dynamically based on context.\n\nThe result? Platforms that maintain themselves.\n\n→ Schemas that evolve without breaking downstream consumers\n→ Pipelines that self-heal when sources change shape\n→ Access controls that adapt to who's querying and why\n\nThis isn't a future trend. Early adopters at major fintechs and logistics companies are already running production agent loops inside their data platforms.\n\nThe question isn't whether your stack will become intelligent. It's whether you'll architect for it now or bolt it on later.\n\nWhat's the most interesting data infrastructure shift you're seeing in your org? Let's compare notes. ⬇️","visibility":"PUBLIC","distribution":{"feedDistribution":"MAIN_FEED","targetEntities":[],"thirdPartyDistributionChannels":[]},"lifecycleState":"PUBLISHED","isReshareDisabledByAuthor":false}

If your warehouse isn't querying in milliseconds, you're already behind your competitors.\n\n→ 2. The semantic layer revolution\nRaw tables are dying. Smart organizations are abstracting their metrics into semantic layers so AI agents and business stakeholders speak the same language. dbt, Cube, AtScale — pick your stack and standardize.\n\n→ 3. Data quality becomes decentralized\nNo more data team single point of failure. Domain ownership is the shift. Engineering owns their schemas. Product owns their events. Data teams provide the guardrails and governance.\n\n→ 4. AI-ready infrastructure is non-negotiable\nVector stores, embedding pipelines, retrieval-augmented pipelines — these aren't experimental anymore. They're core architecture decisions every data leader must make this year.\n\n\nThe teams winning in 2025 aren't the ones with the most tools in their stack.\n\nThey're the ones with the cleanest foundations.\n\nStop chasing shiny objects. Double down on quality, semantics, and speed.\n\nWhat's the one trend you're betting your budget on this year? The bottleneck was inevitable. Requests piled up. The data team became the \"no\" department.\n\nA new operating model is quietly taking over, and it's not what you'd expect.\n\n1. DOMAIN-ORIENTED OWNERSHIP\nProduct teams own their data end-to-end. Marketing owns marketing data. Finance owns finance data. The central team becomes a platform — building infrastructure, setting standards, enabling others. Not gatekeeping.\n\n2. DATA CONTRACTS AS THE GLUE\nThis is the real enabler. Teams agree on formal data contracts — schema definitions, SLAs, freshness guarantees. If a source team changes a table, downstream consumers know before anything breaks. Netflix and Uber have done this for years. Now it's accessible to everyone.\n\n3. EMBEDDED ANALYSTS OVER CENTRALIZED REPORTING\nInstead of building dashboards from a silo, analysts sit inside product teams. They attend standups, understand the context, and build what's actually needed. The quality difference is night and day.\n\n4. THE PLATFORM TEAM BECOMES A PRODUCT TEAM\nThe central team treats internal infrastructure like a product. They measure adoption, gather feedback, run demos, and ship improvements on a regular cadence. Same product mindset, different users.\n\n→ The companies making this shift aren't just getting better data. They're moving faster as organizations. Data becomes a multiplier, not a bottleneck.\n\nIf your team has started this transition, I'd love to hear what worked. DOMAIN-ORIENTED OWNERSHIP\nProduct teams own their data end-to-end. Marketing owns marketing data. Finance owns finance data. The central team becomes a platform — building infrastructure, setting standards, enabling others. Not gatekeeping.\n\n2. DATA CONTRACTS AS THE GLUE\nThis is the real enabler. Teams agree on formal data contracts — schema definitions, SLAs, freshness guarantees. If a source team changes a table, downstream consumers know before anything breaks. Netflix and Uber have done this for years. Now it's accessible to everyone.\n\n3. EMBEDDED ANALYSTS OVER CENTRALIZED REPORTING\nInstead of building dashboards from a silo, analysts sit inside product teams. They attend standups, understand the context, and build what's actually needed. The quality difference is night and day.\n\n4. THE PLATFORM TEAM BECOMES A PRODUCT TEAM\nThe central team treats internal infrastructure like a product. They measure adoption, gather feedback, run demos, and ship improvements on a regular cadence. Same product mindset, different users.\n\n→ The companies making this shift aren't just getting better data. They're moving faster as organizations. Data becomes a multiplier, not a bottleneck.\n\nIf your team has started this transition, I'd love to hear what worked. A lightweight router LLM classifies the incoming query and sends it to the right module\n2. A retrieval system pulls relevant context from your knowledge base (RAG, vector search, or hybrid)\n3. They're designing modular AI architectures that fit their existing data pipelines.\n\nThe era of 'one model to rule them all' is over. Specialized, composable, and debuggable is the new standard.\n\nAre you building compound systems or still betting on a single model? They spend 40% of their week fixing broken pipelines, debugging schema changes they weren't told about, and fielding Slack DMs about \"why are the numbers different?\"\n\nHere's what's changing.\n\nThe industry is shifting from reactive data quality to proactive data contracts. And it's about time.\n\nA data contract is exactly what it sounds like — a formal agreement between data producers (source systems, engineers) and data consumers (analysts, scientists). It defines:\n\n1. Schema guarantees — What fields exist, their types, and which are nullable\n2. Freshness SLAs — How recent the data must be (hourly, daily, weekly)\n3. Semantic meaning — What each metric actually means (no more \"we define churn differently\")\n4. Ownership — Who to ping when something breaks at 2 AM\n\nCompanies like Netflix, Airbnb, and Uber have been practicing this internally for years. Now tools like dbt contracts, Great Expectations, and Soda are making it accessible to teams of any size.\n\nThe result? Teams adopting contracts see 60% fewer data incidents within the first quarter. I've witnessed this firsthand with two clients this quarter alone.\n\nThe hardest part is not the technology. It is the culture shift — getting producers to commit to guarantees when they are used to deploying schema changes freely.\n\n→ Start with your 3 most critical source tables\n→ Define the contract collaboratively, not unilaterally\n→ Enforce it in CI/CD before it reaches production\n→ Review and iterate monthly with stakeholders\n\nThe teams that nail this won't just have cleaner data. They spend 40% of their week fixing broken pipelines, debugging schema changes they weren't told about, and fielding Slack DMs about \"why are the numbers different?\"\n\nHere's what's changing.\n\nThe industry is shifting from reactive data quality to proactive data contracts. And it's about time.\n\nA data contract is exactly what it sounds like — a formal agreement between data producers (source systems, engineers) and data consumers (analysts, scientists). It defines:\n\n1. Schema guarantees — What fields exist, their types, and which are nullable\n2. Freshness SLAs — How recent the data must be (hourly, daily, weekly)\n3. Semantic meaning — What each metric actually means (no more \"we define churn differently\")\n4. Ownership — Who to ping when something breaks at 2 AM\n\nCompanies like Netflix, Airbnb, and Uber have been practicing this internally for years. Now tools like dbt contracts, Great Expectations, and Soda are making it accessible to teams of any size.\n\nThe result? Teams adopting contracts see 60% fewer data incidents within the first quarter. I've witnessed this firsthand with two clients this quarter alone.\n\nThe hardest part is not the technology. It is the culture shift — getting producers to commit to guarantees when they are used to deploying schema changes freely.\n\n→ Start with your 3 most critical source tables\n→ Define the contract collaboratively, not unilaterally\n→ Enforce it in CI/CD before it reaches production\n→ Review and iterate monthly with stakeholders\n\nThe teams that nail this won't just have cleaner data. They will ship faster, earn trust from leadership, and finally stop firefighting.\n\nIs your data on a contract, or is it still the Wild West? ♻️","visibility":"PUBLIC","distribution":{"feedDistribution":"MAIN_FEED","targetEntities":[],"thirdPartyDistributionChannels":[]},"lifecycleState":"PUBLISHED","isReshareDisabledByAuthor":false}

Data Mesh Goes Mainstream\nEnterprises are dismantling monolithic data platforms. Individual business domains now own, serve, and maintain their own data products. The result? Teams actually care about quality because it's THEIR reputation on the line. Faster discovery, fewer bottlenecks, sharper accountability.\n\n2. Contracts Over Catalogs\nSnowflake, Databricks, and ThoughtSpot are racing to build better discovery layers. But the real unlock isn't more metadata — it's enforceable contracts. The new bottleneck is governance, discovery, and data culture.\n\nTeams investing in product thinking (not just pipeline engineering) right now are going to define the next decade of analytics.\n\nWhat's your data org's north star — mesh, lakehouse, or something hybrid? But they're discovering a hard truth — without a semantic layer, your AI is just guessing.\n\nThe semantic layer isn't a new idea. Looker popularized it. dbt brought it to analytics. But the 2025 twist is different: it's now the bridge between structured data AND LLMs.\n\nHere's what changed:\n\n1. LLMs can't query raw schemas. Column names like 'cust_attr_47' mean nothing to an AI. A semantic layer translates cryptic jargon into business language a model can understand. Your data becomes fluent in 'human.'\n\n2. Governance isn't optional anymore. When non-technical teams ask AI questions about revenue data, you need a controlled surface area. The semantic layer becomes your security perimeter — not a bottleneck.\n\n3. Consistent metrics at scale. I recently audited a company where 'active user' meant 4 completely different things across departments. A semantic layer enforces one truth that both humans AND machines reference.\n\n4. Cross-platform portability. Write your business logic once in the semantic layer. It powers your dashboards, your AI chatbots, your embedded analytics — all from the same definitions.\n\nThe teams winning this year aren't the ones with the most sophisticated ML stacks. They're the ones who can describe their data well enough for AI to actually use it.\n\nMetadata isn't boring compliance work anymore. It's your competitive moat.\n\nIs your team investing in semantic layers yet? How are you structuring data for AI consumption? Here are the three shifts that keep coming up:\n\n1. THE AI-ENABLED ANALYST IS REPLACING THE PURE SQL JOCKEY\nForward-looking orgs now pair every 2-3 analysts with an AI engineer. The analysts handle prompt engineering, validation, and business context — the AI handles query generation and pattern detection. Early adopters report 3x analysis velocity and deeper insights, not just faster queries.\n\n2. DATA PLATFORM → DATA PRODUCT MINDSET\nThe biggest shift I'm watching: teams moving from 'build a pipeline and hope they use it' to treating every dataset like a product. That means SLAs, versioning, changelogs, user feedback loops, and deprecation notices. The companies winning at data right now don't just move data — they serve it with intent.\n\n3. Organizations spent 5 years building lakes, warehouses, and pipelines. Now leadership wants receipts.\n\n2. Data monetization is no longer a side project. I'm tracking companies that have moved from internal analytics to selling data products — APIs, datasets, benchmarks — as revenue streams. Databricks just reported their consumption-based revenue grew 60% YoY. The market is voting with its wallet.\n\n3. The role is shifting from \"data plumber\" to \"data product manager.\" The teams getting funded aren't the ones with the best tech stack. They're the ones who can articulate: this pipeline feeds this dashboard which saves this team X hours per week, valued at Y.\n\nThe uncomfortable truth? Most data orgs can't answer basic unit economics questions.\n\nOne fintech I work with reframed their entire data org around \"revenue per query.\" Every dataset, every model, every dashboard got a cost-to-value score. They cut 40% of their scheduled queries and nobody noticed. The ones they kept? High-value, high-usage. That's the benchmark now.\n\nWhat's your data org's north star metric?\nIf you had to attach a dollar value to your last dashboard, what would it be?\n\nThe data gold rush is over. You look at charts after the fact.\n→ Data-fluent = embedded. Data flows into every decision organically.\n→ Data-driven = dashboards people ignore by Tuesday.\n→ Data-fluent = teams that instinctively ask \"what does the data say?\"\n\nThe trend reshaping the industry right now:\n\n1. Companies are moving away from centralized data teams as gatekeepers\n2. Instead, they're embedding data literacy programs across departments\n3. The metric that matters: time from question to insight, not dashboard count\n\nI recently spoke with a Head of Data at a fintech company who cut their insight delivery window from 2 weeks to 4 hours. Not by hiring more analysts. By changing how teams interact with data at every level.\n\nThe playbook emerging:\n\n→ Stop building more dashboards. Build decision workflows instead.\n→ Teach critical thinking first, SQL syntax second.\n→ Make your data team coaches, not order-takers.\n\nThe companies winning right now aren't the ones with the most data. They're the ones where every employee can ask the right question.\n\nIs your organization data-rich but insight-poor?\n\nWhat's the one structural change that would make the biggest difference? Let me push back on that.\n\nHere's what's really happening in the enterprise data space right now.\n\n→ Domain ownership is finally working — but not how the textbooks predicted\n→ Centralized teams aren't disappearing, they're evolving into platform enablers\n→ The real bottleneck? Organizational maturity, not technology\n\nThree shifts I'm tracking closely heading into Q3:\n\n1. COMPOSABLE DATA STACKS\nTeams are ditching monolithic platforms for interchangeable components. Catalog here, query engine there, governance somewhere else. The glue is open standards — Iceberg, Delta Lake, Unity Catalog extensions. If your stack can't swap components, you're locked in.\n\n2. SEMANTIC LAYERS MAKE A COMEBACK\nAfter years of \"just let everyone query raw data,\" the pendulum is swinging back. Universal semantic layers — think dbt Metrics, Cube, or even LookML — are becoming the single source of truth for business definitions. Analysts get consistency. Engineers get control.\n\n3. AI-ENHANCED DATA CONTRACTS\nWe're seeing teams pair traditional data contracts (schemas, SLAs, ownership) with AI agents that proactively monitor drift. When a source schema changes, the agent flags downstream consumers before anything breaks, not after. This shifts data ops from reactive to proactive.\n\nThe companies winning aren't the ones with the best tools. Catalog here, query engine there, governance somewhere else. The glue is open standards — Iceberg, Delta Lake, Unity Catalog extensions. If your stack can't swap components, you're locked in.\n\n2. SEMANTIC LAYERS MAKE A COMEBACK\nAfter years of \"just let everyone query raw data,\" the pendulum is swinging back. Universal semantic layers — think dbt Metrics, Cube, or even LookML — are becoming the single source of truth for business definitions. Analysts get consistency. Engineers get control.\n\n3. AI-ENHANCED DATA CONTRACTS\nWe're seeing teams pair traditional data contracts (schemas, SLAs, ownership) with AI agents that proactively monitor drift. When a source schema changes, the agent flags downstream consumers before anything breaks, not after. This shifts data ops from reactive to proactive.\n\nThe companies winning aren't the ones with the best tools. They're the ones who figured out that data architecture is 20% technology and 80% organizational change management.\n\nWhat trend are you tracking right now that nobody's talking about yet? Embedded analytics is becoming table stakes — not dashboards in a BI tool, but insights surfaced inside Slack, email drafts, CRM records, where decisions actually happen.\n\n2. The rise of the \"decision engineer\" — a hybrid role combining analytics engineering, product thinking, and domain expertise. Not just building pipelines but designing decision workflows.\n\n3. Governance is flipping from restrictive to enabling — instead of \"you can't see this data,\" forward-thinking teams say \"here's the data with context, caveats, and a confidence score attached.\"\n\nThe next competitive advantage isn't more data. It's faster, better-informed decisions at every level of the org.\n\nIs your team optimizing for query speed or decision speed? SQL Is Making a Comeback — But Differently\nNot the SELECT * FROM days. Modern SQL-first workflows with dbt, DuckDB, and Iceberg are pulling analytical power back from Python-dominated pipelines. Simpler stacks, fewer failure points, faster iteration.\n\nThe bottom line: the age of the data unicorn is over. The age of the data specialist is here. Data teams are adopting a product mindset.\n\nHere's what's changing:\n\n1. From requests to discovery → Instead of \"build me this dashboard,\" teams are asking \"what decisions are being made?\" and designing backward from outcomes.\n\n2. From one-off outputs to reusable assets → That SQL query you wrote last month? Package it as a data product with SLAs, documentation, and ownership. Treat it like software.\n\n3. From batch to real-time → The latency expectation has shifted from \"give me last week's numbers\" to \"what's happening right now?\" Streaming patterns are becoming table stakes.\n\n4. From ownership to enablement → The best data teams don't hoard access — they build data marketplaces where domain teams can self-serve with quality guarantees.\n\nThe data mesh conversation was the start. But the real signal is simpler: data is being treated as a product, not a byproduct.\n\n→ Companies winning at data right now aren't the ones with the most tools. They're the ones treating their data like they treat their customer-facing products — with curation, iteration, and clear value propositions.\n\nWhat's your data team's biggest challenge in making this shift?
